source: "Schwartz's Principles of Surgery, 11th Edition"

# CHAPTER 22 <span style="color:#22d3ee">THORACIC</span> AORTIC ANEURYSMS AND AORTIC <span style="color:#f87171">DISSECTION</span> 胸主動脈瘤與主動脈剝離

source: Schwartz's Principles of Surgery, 11th Edition

---

## <span style="color:#60a5fa;font-weight:600">Thoracic Aortic Aneurysm (TAA) 胸主動脈瘤</span>

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Etiology & Classification 病因與分類</span>

| Cause 病因 | Features 特徵 |
|-----------|--------------|
| **Nonspecific medial degeneration** 非特異性中膜退化 | Most common cause; elastic fiber fragmentation, smooth muscle loss 最常見原因；彈力纖維斷裂、平滑肌流失 |
| **Aortic dissection** 主動服剝離 | Creates false lumen, weakens wall 形成假腔、弱化管壁 |
| **Marfan syndrome** 馬凡氏症候群 | FBN1 mutation, fibrillin abnormality, TGF-β excess |
| **Loeys-Dietz syndrome** | TGF-β receptor mutations; aggressive, low threshold for repair |
| **Ehlers-Danlos (vascular type)** 埃勒斯-當洛氏(血管型) | Type III collagen defect; spontaneous arterial rupture 第三型膠原蛋白缺陷；自發性动脉破裂 |
| **Familial TAA** 家族性TAA | >20% have genetic predisposition; ACTA2 (14% of families) |
| **Bicuspid aortic valve** 雙葉主動服瓣 | 2% of population; 10x higher dissection risk; medial degeneration |
| **Infection (mycotic)** 感染(徽菌性) | S. aureus, Salmonella; saccular aneurysms |
| **Syphilitic aortitis** 梅毒性主動服炎 | Treponema pallidum; obliterative endarteritis of vasa vasorum |
| **Aortitis** 主動服炎 | Takayasu, giant cell arteritis, rheumatoid |
| **Trauma (pseudoaneurysm)** 外傷(假性動瘤) | Contained leak from trauma/surgery 外傷/術後局限性滲漏 |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Growth & Rupture Risk 生長與破裂風險</span>

| Location 位置 | Growth Rate 生長速率 | Critical Diameter 臨界直徑 | Rupture Risk at Critical 臨界時破裂風險 |
|--------------|-------------------|------------------------|-------------------------------------|
| **Ascending aorta** 升主動服 | 0.07 cm/yr | 6.0 cm | 31% |
| **Descending aorta** 降主動服 | 0.19 cm/yr | 7.0 cm | 43% |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Aneurysm >6cm Annual Rates >6公分主動服瘤年度比率</span>

- **Rupture 破裂:** 3.6%
- **<span style="color:#f87171">Dissection</span> 剝離:** 3.7%
- **Death 死亡:** 10.8%

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Clinical Manifestations 臨床表現</span>

| Presentation 表現 | Features 特徵 |
|-----------------|--------------|
| **Asymptomatic** 無症狀 | Incidental finding on imaging 影像檢查時意外發現 |
| **Chest discomfort** 胸部不適 | Anterior chest, mimics angina (ascending) 前胸，像心絞痛(升主動服) |
| **Back pain** 背痛 | Between scapulae (descending) 肩胛骨間(降主動服) |
| **Compression symptoms** 壓迫症狀 | SVC syndrome, hoarseness (RLN), dysphagia, hemoptysis |
| **Aortic regurgitation** 主動服逆流 | Annular dilatation, heart failure 環狀擴張、心衰竭 |
| **Embolization** 栓塞 | Atheromatous debris to visceral/renal/extremities 粥狀硬化碎屑至內臟/腎/肢體 |
| **Rupture** 破裂 | Cardiac tamponade (ascending), hemorrhagic shock (descending) |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Imaging 影像學</span>

| Modality 工具 | Use 用途 |
|--------------|---------|
| **CXR** X光 | Initial screening; may appear normal 初步篩檢；可能正常 |
| **Echo (TTE/TEE)** 超音 | Ascending aorta, aortic root, valve; intraoperative 升主動服、主動服根部、瓣膜；術中 |
| **CT angiography** CTA | Gold standard; 3D reconstruction; entire aorta 黃金標準；3D重建；整條主動服 |
| **MRI/MRA** | Excellent detail; no contrast (renal failure patients) 極佳解析；無對比劑(腎衰竭患者) |
| **Angiography** 血管攝影 | Definitive anatomy; branch vessel involvement 確定解剖；分支血管侵犯 |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Standard Aortic Measurement Locations 標準主動服測量位置</span>

1. Sinuses of Valsalva 瓦爾沙瓦實
2. Sinotubular junction 竇管交界
3. Mid-ascending <span style="color:#22d3ee">aorta</span> 中段升主動服
4. Proximal arch (innominate origin) 近端主動弓(無名動脉起始)
5. Mid-arch (between carotid/<span style="color:#22d3ee">subclavian</span>) 中段主動弓(頸/鎖骨下之間)
6. Proximal descending (isthmus) 近端降主動服(峽部)
7. Mid-descending 中段降主動服
8. <span style="color:#22d3ee">Aorta</span> at <span style="color:#22d3ee">diaphragm</span> 橫膈膜處主動服
9. Abdominal <span style="color:#22d3ee">aorta</span> (celiac origin) 腹主動服(腹腔軸起始)

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Bicuspid Aortic Valve Considerations 雙葉主動服瓣考量</span>

- 50-70% develop aortic dilatation 50-70%會發展主動服擴張
- Consider ascending <span style="color:#22d3ee">aorta</span> replacement at **>4.5 cm** during AVR 建議主動服瓣置換時一併置換>4.5公分的升主動服
- Different pathobiology than tricuspid valve aneurysms 與三葉瓣動服瘤有不同的病理生物學

---

## <span style="color:#60a5fa;font-weight:600">Aortic Dissection 主動服剝離</span>

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Stanford Classification 斯坦福分類</span>

| Type 類型 | Description 描述 |
|----------|----------------|
| **Type A** | Involves ascending aorta (with or without arch/descending) 侵犯升主動服(含或不含主動弓/降主動服) |
| **Type B** | Involves only descending aorta (distal to L subclavian) 只侵犯降主動服(左鎖骨下动脉遠端) |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">DeBakey Classification 德貝基分類</span>

| Type 類型 | Description 描述 |
|----------|----------------|
| **Type I** | Involves entire aorta 侵犯整條主動服 |
| **Type II** | 局限於升主動服 |
| **Type IIIa** | 局限於胸降主動服 |
| **Type IIIb** | 延伸至腹主動服 |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Pathophysiology 病理生理學</span>

1. **Intimal tear** 內膜裂口 → blood enters media 血液進入中膜
2. **Medial separation** 中膜分離 → creates true + false lumen 形成真腔+假腔
3. **Propagation** 延伸 → branch vessel compromise 分支血管受影響
4. **Weakening** 弱化 → rupture risk 破裂風險

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Risk Factors 危險因子</span>

- **Hypertension** (most common; 70% of patients) 高血壓(最常見；70%患者)
- Connective tissue disorders (Marfan, Loeys-Dietz, EDS) 結締組織疾病
- Bicuspid aortic valve 雙葉主動服瓣
- Cocaine use 使用古柯鹼
- Trauma 外傷
- Iatrogenic (catheterization, cardiac surgery) 醫源性

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Clinical Presentation 臨床表現</span>

| Finding 發現 | Description 描述 |
|-------------|----------------|
| **Sudden severe chest pain** 突發性劇烈胸痛 | "Tearing" or "ripping" quality "撕裂"或"撕裂"性質 |
| **Pain radiation** 疼痛放射 | To back, neck, jaw, abdomen 至背部、頸部、下頜、腹部 |
| **Hypertension** 高血壓 | More common in Type B B型較常見 |
| **Hypotension** 低血壓 | Suggests rupture, tamponade, severe AR (Type A) 提示破裂、填塞、嚴重AR(A型) |
| **Pulse deficit** 脈搏缺失 | Carotid, brachial, femoral (30%) 頸、肱、股動脉(30%) |
| **New murmur** 新雜音 | Aortic regurgitation (Type A) 主動服逆流(A型) |
| **CVA/stroke** 中風 | Cerebral hypoperfusion 腦部低灌流 |
| **Paraplegia** 下身麻痺 | Spinal cord ischemia 脊髓缺血 |
| **Mesenteric ischemia** 腸繫膜缺血 | Abdominal pain, organ dysfunction 腹痛、器官功能障礙 |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Diagnostic Imaging 診斷影像</span>

| Test 檢查 | Recommendation 建議 |
|----------|------------------|
| **CT angiography** CTA | First-line; sensitivity >95%, specificity >95% 首選；敏感度>95%、特異性>95% |
| **TEE** | Bedside; cardiac function, valve, pericardium 床邊；心功能、瓣膜、心包 |
| **MRI** | Excellent detail; longer acquisition time 極佳解析；採集時間較長 |
| **Aortography** 主動服攝影 | Historical gold standard; rarely needed now 歷史黃金標準；現已少用 |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Type A Dissection Management A型剝離處理</span>

| Situation 情況 | Treatment 治療 |
|--------------|------------|
| **Acute Type A** 急性A型 | **Emergency surgical repair** (ascending aorta replacement) 緊急手術修復(升主動服置換) |
| **Complicated Type A** 複雜性A型 | (Malperfusion, rupture) → Emergency surgery (灌注不良、破裂)→緊急手術 |
| **Uncomplicated** 無併發症 | Ascending aorta replacement ± arch repair 升主動服置換±主動弓修復 |
| **Malperfusion syndrome** 灌注不良症候群 | May need hybrid approach 可能需要混合方式 |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Type B Dissection Management B型剝離處理</span>

| Situation 情況 | Treatment 治療 |
|--------------|------------|
| **Uncomplicated** 無併發症 | Medical management (BP control, monitoring) 藥物治療(血壓控制、監測) |
| **Complicated** 複雜性 | (rupture, malperfusion, expansion) → TEVAR or open repair |
| **Chronic (>14 days)** 慢性(>14天) | Medical management; surgery for complications |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Medical Management Goals 藥物治療目標</span>

- **HR:** <60 bpm 心率<60
- **SBP:** 100-120 mmHg 收縮壓100-120
- **IV beta-blockers** (esmolol, labetalol) first-line 靜脈β受體阻斷劑優先
- **Add vasodilators** (nitroprusside) if needed 必要時加用血管擴張劑

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Surgical/Endovascular Options 手術/血管內治療選項</span>

| Procedure 術式 | Use 用途 |
|--------------|---------|
| **Ascending aorta replacement** 升主動服置換 | Type A |
| **Hemiarch repair** 半弓修復 | Limited arch involvement 有限主動弓侵犯 |
| **Total arch replacement + frozen elephant trunk** 全弓置換+冰凍象鼻 | Extended arch disease 廣泛主動弓疾病 |
| **TEVAR** | Type B complicated; preferred over open 複雜性B型；優於開放手術 |
| **Open descending aorta replacement** 開放式降主動服置換 | Failed TEVAR, contraindications TEVAR失敗、禁忌症 |

---

## <span style="color:#60a5fa;font-weight:600">Heritable Aortic Syndromes 遺傳性主動服症候群</span>

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Marfan Syndrome 馬凡氏症候群</span>

| Feature 特徵 | Detail 詳情 |
|------------|-----------|
| **Gene** 基因 | FBN1 (chromosome 15) |
| **Protein** 蛋白質 | Fibrillin-1 |
| **Mechanism** 機制 | Abnormal fibrillin → ↑TGF-β activity → matrix degeneration |
| **Cardiac** 心臟 | Aortic root dilatation (75-85%), annuloaortic ectasia, MVP |
| **Extracardiac** 心臟外 | Tall stature, high palate, joint hypermobility, lens dislocation |
| **Surgical threshold** 手術閾值 | Ascending aorta >5.0-5.5 cm (varies by institution) |
| **Life expectancy** 預期壽命 | 32 years without treatment; now near normal with surgery |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Loeys-Dietz Syndrome</span>

| Feature 特徵 | Detail 詳情 |
|------------|-----------|
| **Gene** 基因 | TGFBR1, TGFBR2 |
| **Triad** 三聯徵 | Arterial tortuosity/aneurysms + hypertelorism + bifid uvula/cleft palate |
| **Behavior** 行為 | Aggressive; rupture/dissection at smaller diameters |
| **Surgical threshold** 手術閾值 | Aorta >4.0-4.5 cm (lower than other syndromes) |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Ehlers-Danlos (Vascular Type) 埃勒斯-當洛氏(血管型)</span>

| Feature 特徵 | Detail 詳情 |
|------------|-----------|
| **Gene** 基因 | COL3A1 (type III collagen) |
| **Problem** 問題 | Spontaneous arterial rupture (mesenteric most common) |
| **Aortic** 主動服 | TAA and dissection less common, but very challenging surgery |
| **Surgical caution** 手術注意 | Tissue integrity severely reduced; holds sutures poorly |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Familial TAA 家族性胸主動服瘤</span>

- **>20% of TAA patients** have genetic predisposition >20%胸主動服瘤患者有遺傳傾向
- **ACTA2 mutation** (14% of families)
- **Autosomal dominant** with variable penetrance 體染色體顯性，表現度各異
- Other genes: MYH11, MYLK, ELN, TGFBR1/2, SMAD3, FBN2, etc.

---

## <span style="color:#60a5fa;font-weight:600">Surgical Techniques 手術技術</span>

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Ascending Aortic Replacement 升主動服置換</span>

- **Standard approach:** Median sternotomy, CPB 標準方式：正中胸骨切開、體外循環
- **Cannulation:** Axillary <span style="color:#22d3ee">artery</span> (best for arch surgery), femoral, direct aortic 插管：腋動脉(主動弓手術最佳)、股動脉、直接主動服
- **Myocardial protection:** Antegrade/retrograde cardioplegia 心肌保護：順行/逆行心麻痹
- **Valve-sparing root replacement (David procedure):** When valve salvage possible 保留瓣膜根部置換：當可保留瓣膜時

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Aortic Arch Replacement 主動弓置換</span>

| Technique 技術 | Description 描述 |
|--------------|----------------|
| **Hemiarch** 半弓 | Replace ascending + proximal arch 置換升主動服+近端主動弓 |
| **Total arch** 全弓 | Replace entire arch with individual branch reattachment 置換整個主動弓並個別重新連接分支 |
| **Frozen elephant trunk** 冰凍象鼻 | Arch replacement + stented descending graft (hybrid) 主動弓置換+支架型降主動服移植片(混合) |
| **Debranching** 分支轉移 | Arch branch vessels bypassed to allow TEVAR 主動弓分支繞道以進行TEVAR |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Descending Thoracic Aortic Replacement 降胸主動服置換</span>

- **Left <span style="color:#60a5fa">thoracotomy</span>** approach 左胸切開方式
- **CPB:** Often left heart bypass or femoral-femoral 體外循環：常為左心繞道或股-股
- **Spinal protection:** CSF drainage, mild hypothermia, spinal monitoring 脊髓保護：腦脊髓液引流、輕度低溫、脊髓監測
- **Renal/<span style="color:#22d3ee">visceral</span> protection:** Cold perfusion 腎/臟器保護：冷灌注

---

## <span style="color:#60a5fa;font-weight:600">Endovascular Repair (TEVAR) 血管內修復</span>

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Indications 適應症</span>

- **Complicated Type B <span style="color:#f87171">dissection</span>** 複雜性B型剝離
- **Traumatic aortic injury** (pseudoaneurysm) 外傷性主動服損傷
- **Penetrating aortic <span style="color:#f87171">ulcer</span>** 穿透性主動服潰瘤
- **High-risk TAA** (selected cases) 高風險胸主動服瘤(特定病例)

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Advantages 優點</span>

- Less invasive than open surgery 比開放手術創傷小
- Lower perioperative <span style="color:#60a5fa">mortality</span> 術期死亡率較低
- Shorter ICU/hospital stay ICU/住院時間較短
- Less spinal cord injury risk 脊髓損傷風險較低

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Complications 併發症</span>

| Complication 併發症 | Risk 風險 | Prevention/Treatment 預防/治療 |
|-------------------|----------|------------------------------|
| **Endoleak** 內漏 | 5-15% | Type I: proximal extension; Type II: embolization |
| **Retrograde Type A dissection** 逆行性A型剝離 | 2-5% | Careful wire/catheter manipulation |
| **Spinal cord ischemia** 脊髓缺血 | 3-5% | CSF drainage, staged procedures |
| **Stroke** 中風 | 2-5% | Careful arch manipulation |
| **Renal failure** 腎衰竭 | 5-10% | Hydration, minimize contrast |

---

## <span style="color:#60a5fa;font-weight:600">Thoracoabdominal Aortic Aneurysm (TAAA) 胸腹主動服瘤</span>

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Crawford Classification 克勞福分類</span>

| Type 類型 | Extent 範圍 |
|----------|-----------|
| **Type I** | Descending胸 → suprarenal abdominal 降主動服→腎上腹主動服 |
| **Type II** | Descending胸 → infrarenal abdominal 降主動服→腎下腹主動服 |
| **Type III** | Lower descending → infrarenal 下段降主動服→腎下 |
| **Type IV** | Infrarenal (juxtarenal) only 僅腎下(近腎) |
| **Type V** | Supra celiac/pre celiac 腹腔軸上方/前 |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Surgical Strategy 手術策略</span>

- **Left thoracoabdominal incision** 左胸腹切開
- **CPB:** Left heart bypass, femoral-femoral 體外循環：左心繞道、股-股
- **<span style="color:#22d3ee">Visceral</span> protection:** Cold Ringer's lactate or blood cardioplegia into branch vessels 臟器保護：冷林格氏乳酸鹽或血液心麻痹液注入分支血管
- **Spinal protection:** CSF drainage, hypothermia, reimplant intercostals 脊髓保護：腦脊髓液引流、低溫、重新植入肋間血管

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Complications 併發症</span>

| Complication 併發症 | Risk 風險 |
|-------------------|----------|
| **Spinal cord ischemia/paraplegia** 脊髓缺血/下身麻痺 | 3-8% (Type I/II higher) |
| **Acute kidney injury** 急性腎損傷 | 10-20% |
| **Respiratory failure** 呼吸衰竭 | 10-15% |
| **Mortality** 死亡率 | 5-15% (higher in Type II) |

---

## <span style="color:#60a5fa;font-weight:600">Postoperative Care 術後照護</span>

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Monitoring 監測</span>

- **Hemodynamic:** Invasive BP, CVP, sometimes PA catheter 血流動力學：有創血壓、中心靜脈壓、有時肺動脉導管
- **Neurologic:** Mental status, motor/sensory exam, spinal drain 神經學：意識狀態、運動/感覺檢查、脊髓引流管
- **Renal:** Urine output, creatinine 腎臟：尿量、肌酸酐
- **Cardiac:** ECG, troponin, echo for EF/valve function 心臟：心電圖、肌鈣蛋白、超音心圖測EF/瓣膜功能
- **Respiratory:** ABG, chest X-ray 呼吸：血液氣體、胸部X光

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Complications to Watch 需注意的併發症</span>

| Complication 併發症 | Signs 徵象 | Treatment 治療 |
|-------------------|----------|-------------|
| **Bleeding** 出血 | Chest tube output, shock 胸管輸出、休克 | Re-exploration 再開刀 |
| **Tamponade** 填塞 | Beck's triad, echo 貝克氏三聯、超音 | Pericardiocentesis, re-exploration 心包穿刺、再開刀 |
| **Stroke** 中風 | Focal deficits 局部缺損 | CT, neurology consult |
| **Spinal cord injury** 脊髓損傷 | Lower extremity weakness 下肢無力 | CSF drainage, BP augmentation |
| **AR (new/worsened)** 新發/惡化AR | Murmur, HF 雜音、心衰竭 | Echo, possible reoperation |
| **Graft infection** 移植片感染 | Fever, sepsis 發燒、敗血症 | Antibiotics, possible graft excision |

### <span style="color:#22d3ee;font-weight:500">Long-term Follow-up 長期追蹤</span>

- **Imaging surveillance:** CT/MRI yearly 影像監測：每年CT/MRI
- **BP control:** <130/80 mmHg 血壓控制：<130/80
- **Beta-blockers:** Standard 標準使用β受體阻斷劑
- **Avoid strenuous activity:** Heavy lifting in heritable syndromes 避免劇烈活動：遺傳性症候群避免提重物

Coselli JS, Volguina IV , LeMaire SA, <span style="color:#e2e8f0">et al.</span> Early and 1-year outcomes of aortic root surgery in patients with Marfan syndrome: a prospective, multicenter, comparative study. <span style="color:#e2e8f0">J Thorac Cardiovasc Surg</span>. <span style="color:#f87171">2014</span>;147(6):<span style="color:#f87171">1758</span>-<span style="color:#f87171">1766</span>, <span style="color:#f87171">1767</span>.e<span style="color:#f87171">1751</span>-e<span style="color:#f87171">1754</span>. This report details the early and 1-year outcomes of an international prospective study comparing valve-sparing and valve-replacing root operations in patients with Marfan syndrome. Although patients in both groups had excellent early outcomes, those who underwent valve-sparing procedures had a higher incidence of late aortic valve regur- gitation, raising concerns about long-term durability.

Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 897 27/02/19 4:13 PM 898 Table 23-1 Pertinent elements in vascular history • History of stroke or transient ischemic attack • History of coronary artery disease, including previous myocardial infarction and angina • History of peripheral arterial disease • History of diabetes • History of hypertension • History of tobacco use • History of hyperlipidemia GENERAL APPROACH TO THE VASCULAR PATIENT Since the vascular system involves every organ system in our body, the symptoms of vascular disease are as varied as those encountered in any medical specialty. Lack of adequate blood supply to target organs typically presents with pain, for exam- ple, calf pain with lower extremity claudication, postprandial abdominal pain from mesenteric ischemia, and arm pain with axillo-subclavian arterial occlusion. In contrast, stroke and tran- sient ischemic attack (TIA) are the presenting symptoms from middle cerebral embolization as a consequence of a stenosed internal carotid artery. The pain syndrome of arterial disease is usually divided clinically into acute and chronic types, with all shades of severity between the two extremes. Sudden onset of pain can indicate complete occlusion of a critical vessel, leading to mo re se vere pa in an d cr itical is chemia in th e ta rget organ, resulting in lower limb gangrene or intestinal infarction. Chronic pain results from a slower, more progressive atheroscle- rotic occlusion, whic h can be tota lly or par tially comp ensated by developing collateral vessels. Acute on chronic is another pain pattern in which a patient most likely has an underlying arterial stenosis that suddenly occludes, for example, the patient with a history of calf claudication who now presents with sudden, severe acute limb-threatening ischemia. The clinician should always try to understand and relate the clinical manifestations to the underlying pathologic process. The Vascular History Appropriate history should be focused based on the present- ing symptoms re lated to th e va scular sy stem (T able 23 -1). Of particular importance in the previous medical history is noting prior vascular interventions (endovascular or open surgical), and all vascular patients should have inquiry made about their prior cardiac history an d cu rrent ca rdiac sy mptoms. Ap proximately 30% of vascular patients will be diabetic. A history of prior and current smoking status should be noted. The patient with carotid disease in most cases is com- pletely asymptomatic, having been referred based on the find- ing of a cervical bruit or duplex finding of stenosis. Symptoms of carotid territory TIAs include transient monocular blindness (amaurosis), contralateral weakness or numbness, and dys- phasia. Symptoms persisting longer than 24 hours constitute a stroke. In contrast, the patient with chronic mesenteric isch- emia is likely to present with postprandial abdominal pain and weight loss. The patient fears eating because of the pain, avoids food, and loses weight. It is very unlikely that a patient with abdominal pain who has not lost weight has chronic mesenteric ischemia. The patient with lower extremity pain on ambulation has intermittent claudication that occurs in certain muscle groups; for example, calf pain upon exercise usually reflects superficial femoral artery disease, while pain in the buttocks reflects iliac disease. In most cases, the pain manifests in one muscle group below the level of the affected artery, occurs only with exercise, and is relieved with rest only to recur at the same location, hence the term “window gazer’s disease.” Rest pain (a manifestation of severe underlying occlusive disease) is constant and occurs in the foot (not the muscle groups), typically at the metatarso- phalangeal junction, and is relieved by dependency. Often the Key Points 1 Carotid intervention as a preventive strategy should be per- formed in patients with 60% or greater symptomatic internal carotid artery stenosis and those with 80% or greater asymp- tomatic internal carotid artery stenosis. Carotid intervention for asymptomatic stenosis betwee n 60% and 79% remain s controversial. The modality of carotid intervention—carotid endarterectomy versus carotid stenting—remains contro- versial; currently, carotid endarterectomy appears to be associated with lower stroke rate with long term durability, whereas carotid stenting is more suitable under certain chal- lenging anatomic or physiologic conditions. 2 Abdominal aortic aneurysms should be repaired when the risk of ru pture, de termined ma inly by an eurysm si ze, exceeds the risk of death due to perioperative complications or concurrent illness. Endovascular repair is associated with less perioperative morbidity and mortality compared with open reconstruction and is preferred in patients with suitable anatomic morphology for stent-graft placement. 3 Treatment objectives for sym ptomatic mes enteric isc hemia are to improve quality of life and prevent bowel infarc- tion. Endovascular intervention with stenting has similar treatment efficacy comparative with less perioperative mor- bidity compared to open mesenteric bypass. Surgical recon- struction has a proven durability and patency rate compared with endovascular intervention. 4 Aortoiliac occlusive disease can be treated with either endovascular me ans or op en re construction, de pending on patient risk stratification, occlusion characteristics, and symptomatology. 5 Claudication is a marker of extensive atherosclerosis and is mainly managed with risk factor modification and pharma- cotherapy. Only 5% of patients with claudication will need intervention because of disabling extremity pain. The 5-year mortality of a patient with claudication approaches 30%. Patients with rest pain or tissue loss need expeditious evalu- ation and vascular reconstruction to ameliorate the severe extremity pain an d pr event li mb lo ss. En dovascular in terven- tion is preferred as the first line of therapy for lower extrem- ity occlusive disease, whereas bypass reconstruction should be considered in failed endovascu lar therapy or long seg- ment femoropopliteal occlusive disease. Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 898 27/02/19 4:13 PM 899 ARTERIAL DISEASECHAPTER 23 Table 23-2 Grading scales for peripheral pulses TRADITIONAL SCALE BASIC SCALE 4+ Normal 2+ Normal 3+ Slightly reduced 1+ Diminished 2+ Markedly reduced 0 Absent 1+ Barely palpable patient is prompted to sleep with their foot hanging off one side of the bed to increase the hydrostatic pressure. The Vascular Physical Examination Specific vascular examination should include abdominal aortic palpation, carotid artery examination, and pulse examination of the lower extremity (femoral, popliteal, posterior tibial, and dorsalis pedis arteries). The abdomen should be palpated for an abdominal aortic aneurysm, detected as an expansile pulse above the level of the umbilicus. It should also be examined for the presence of bruits. Because the aorta typically divides at the level of the umbilicus, an aortic aneurysm is most frequently palpable in the epigastrium. In thin individuals, a normal aortic pulsation is palpable, while in obese patients, even large aor- tic aneurysms may not be detectable. Suspicion of a clinically enlarged aorta should lead to the performance of an ultrasound scan for a more accurate definition of aortic diameter. The carotids should be auscultated for the presence of bruits, although there is a higher correlation with coronary artery disease than underlying carotid stenosis. A bruit at the angle of the mandible is a significant finding, leading to follow- up duplex scanning. The differential diagnosis is a transmitted murmur from a sclerotic or stenotic aortic valve. The carotid is palpable deep to the sternocleidomastoid muscle in the neck. Palpation, however, should be gentle and rarely yields clinically useful information. Upper extremity examination is necessary when an arte- riovenous graft is to be inserted in patients who have symptoms of arm pain with exercise. Thoracic outlet syndrome (TOS) can result in occlusion or aneurysm formation of the subclavian artery. Distal embolization is a manifestation of TOS; conse- quently, the fin gers sho uld be exa mined for sig ns of isc hemia and ulceration. The axillary artery enters the limb below the middle of the clavicle, where it can be palpated in thin patients. It is usually easily palpable in the axilla and medial upper arm. The brachial artery is most easily located at the antecubital fossa immediately medial to the biceps tendon. The radial artery is palpable at the wrist anterior to the radius. For lower extremity vascular examination, the femoral pulse is usually palpable midway between the anterior supe- rior iliac spine and the pubic tubercle. The popliteal artery is palpated in the popliteal fossa with the knee flexed to 45° and the foot supported on th e ex amination ta ble to re lax th e ca lf muscles. Palpation of the popliteal artery is a bimanual tech- nique. Both thumbs are placed on the tibial tuberosity anteriorly, and the fingers are placed into the popliteal fossa between the two heads of the gastrocnemius muscle. The popliteal artery is palpated by compressing it against the posterior aspect of the tibia just below the knee. The posterior tibial pulse is detected by palpation 2 cm posterior to the medial malleolus. The dorsa- lis pedis is detected 1 cm lateral to the hallucis longus extensor tendon, which dorsiflexes the great toe and is clearly visible on the dorsum of the foot. Pulses can be grade d using eithe r the traditional four-point scale or the basic two-point scale system (Table 23-2). The foot should also be carefully examined for pallor on elevation and rubor on dependency, as these findings are indicative of chronic ischemia. Note should also be made of nail changes and loss of hair. Ulceration and other findings specific to disease states are described in relevant sections later in this chapter. After reconstructive vascular surgery, the graft may be available for examination, depending on its type and course. The in situ lower extremity graft runs in the subcutaneous fat and can be palpated along most of its length. A change in pulse quality, aneurysmal enlargement, or a new bruit should be care- fully noted. Axillofemoral grafts, femoral-to-femoral grafts, and arteriovenous access grafts can usually be easily palpated as well. Noninvasive Diagnostic Evaluation of the Vascular Patient Ankle-Brachial Index. There is increasing interest in the use of the ankle-brachial index (ABI) to evaluate patients at risk for cardiovascular events. An ABI less than 0.9 correlates with increased risk of myocardial infarction and indicates significant, although perhaps asymptomatic, underlying peripheral vascular disease. The ABI is determined in the following ways. <span style="color:#e2e8f0">Blood</span> pressure is measured in both upper extremities using the high- est systolic blood pressure as the denominator for the ABI. The ankle pressure is determined by placing a blood pressure cuff above the ankle and measuring the return to flow of the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries using a pencil Doppler probe over each artery. The ratio of the systolic pressure in each ves- sel divided by the highest arm systolic pressure can be used to express the ABI in both the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries (Fig. 23-1). Normal is more than 1. Patients with clau- dication typically have an ABI in the 0.5 to 0.7 range, and those with rest pain are in the 0.3 to 0.5 range. Those with gangrene have an ABI of less than 0.3. These ranges can vary depending on the degree of compressibility of the vessel. The test is less reliable in patients with heavily calcified vessels. Due to non- compressibility, some patients, such as diabetics and those with end-stage renal disease, may have ABI ≥1.40 and require addi- tional noninvasive diagnostic testing to evaluate for peripheral artery disease. Alternative tests include toe-brachial pressures, pulse volume recordings, transcutaneous oxygen measurements, or vascular imaging (duplex ultrasound). Segmental Limb Pressures. By placing serial blood pressure cuffs down the lowe r extr emity and then meas uring the pres sure with a Doppler probe as flow returns to the artery below the cuff, it is possible to de termine se gmental pr essures do wn the leg. This data can then be used to infer the level of the occlu- sion. The systolic pressure at each level is expressed as a ratio, with the highest systolic pressure in the upper extremities as the denominator. Normal segmental pressures commonly show high thigh pressures 20 mmHg or greater in comparison to the brachial artery pressures. The low thigh pressure should be equivalent to brachial pressures. Subsequent pressures should fall by no more than 10 mmHg at each level. A pressure gradient of 20 mmHg between two subsequent levels is usually indica- tive of occlusive disease at that level. The most frequently used index is the ratio of the ankle pressure to the brachial pressure, Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 899 27/02/19 4:13 PM

Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 897 27/02/19 4:13 PM 898 Table 23-1 Pertinent elements in vascular history • History of stroke or transient ischemic attack • History of coronary artery disease, including previous myocardial infarction and angina • History of peripheral arterial disease • History of diabetes • History of hypertension • History of tobacco use • History of hyperlipidemia GENERAL APPROACH TO THE VASCULAR PATIENT Since the vascular system involves every organ system in our body, the symptoms of vascular disease are as varied as those encountered in any medical specialty. Lack of adequate blood supply to target organs typically presents with pain, for exam- ple, calf pain with lower extremity claudication, postprandial abdominal pain from mesenteric ischemia, and arm pain with axillo-subclavian arterial occlusion. In contrast, stroke and tran- sient ischemic attack (TIA) are the presenting symptoms from middle cerebral embolization as a consequence of a stenosed internal carotid artery. The pain syndrome of arterial disease is usually divided clinically into acute and chronic types, with all shades of severity between the two extremes. Sudden onset of pain can indicate complete occlusion of a critical vessel, leading to mo re se vere pa in an d cr itical is chemia in th e ta rget organ, resulting in lower limb gangrene or intestinal infarction. Chronic pain results from a slower, more progressive atheroscle- rotic occlusion, whic h can be tota lly or par tially comp ensated by developing collateral vessels. Acute on chronic is another pain pattern in which a patient most likely has an underlying arterial stenosis that suddenly occludes, for example, the patient with a history of calf claudication who now presents with sudden, severe acute limb-threatening ischemia. The clinician should always try to understand and relate the clinical manifestations to the underlying pathologic process. The Vascular History Appropriate history should be focused based on the present- ing symptoms re lated to th e va scular sy stem (T able 23 -1). Of particular importance in the previous medical history is noting prior vascular interventions (endovascular or open surgical), and all vascular patients should have inquiry made about their prior cardiac history an d cu rrent ca rdiac sy mptoms. Ap proximately 30% of vascular patients will be diabetic. A history of prior and current smoking status should be noted. The patient with carotid disease in most cases is com- pletely asymptomatic, having been referred based on the find- ing of a cervical bruit or duplex finding of stenosis. Symptoms of carotid territory TIAs include transient monocular blindness (amaurosis), contralateral weakness or numbness, and dys- phasia. Symptoms persisting longer than 24 hours constitute a stroke. In contrast, the patient with chronic mesenteric isch- emia is likely to present with postprandial abdominal pain and weight loss. The patient fears eating because of the pain, avoids food, and loses weight. It is very unlikely that a patient with abdominal pain who has not lost weight has chronic mesenteric ischemia. The patient with lower extremity pain on ambulation has intermittent claudication that occurs in certain muscle groups; for example, calf pain upon exercise usually reflects superficial femoral artery disease, while pain in the buttocks reflects iliac disease. In most cases, the pain manifests in one muscle group below the level of the affected artery, occurs only with exercise, and is relieved with rest only to recur at the same location, hence the term “window gazer’s disease.” Rest pain (a manifestation of severe underlying occlusive disease) is constant and occurs in the foot (not the muscle groups), typically at the metatarso- phalangeal junction, and is relieved by dependency. Often the Key Points 1 Carotid intervention as a preventive strategy should be per- formed in patients with 60% or greater symptomatic internal carotid artery stenosis and those with 80% or greater asymp- tomatic internal carotid artery stenosis. Carotid intervention for asymptomatic stenosis betwee n 60% and 79% remain s controversial. The modality of carotid intervention—carotid endarterectomy versus carotid stenting—remains contro- versial; currently, carotid endarterectomy appears to be associated with lower stroke rate with long term durability, whereas carotid stenting is more suitable under certain chal- lenging anatomic or physiologic conditions. 2 Abdominal aortic aneurysms should be repaired when the risk of ru pture, de termined ma inly by an eurysm si ze, exceeds the risk of death due to perioperative complications or concurrent illness. Endovascular repair is associated with less perioperative morbidity and mortality compared with open reconstruction and is preferred in patients with suitable anatomic morphology for stent-graft placement. 3 Treatment objectives for sym ptomatic mes enteric isc hemia are to improve quality of life and prevent bowel infarc- tion. Endovascular intervention with stenting has similar treatment efficacy comparative with less perioperative mor- bidity compared to open mesenteric bypass. Surgical recon- struction has a proven durability and patency rate compared with endovascular intervention. 4 Aortoiliac occlusive disease can be treated with either endovascular me ans or op en re construction, de pending on patient risk stratification, occlusion characteristics, and symptomatology. 5 Claudication is a marker of extensive atherosclerosis and is mainly managed with risk factor modification and pharma- cotherapy. Only 5% of patients with claudication will need intervention because of disabling extremity pain. The 5-year mortality of a patient with claudication approaches 30%. Patients with rest pain or tissue loss need expeditious evalu- ation and vascular reconstruction to ameliorate the severe extremity pain an d pr event li mb lo ss. En dovascular in terven- tion is preferred as the first line of therapy for lower extrem- ity occlusive disease, whereas bypass reconstruction should be considered in failed endovascu lar therapy or long seg- ment femoropopliteal occlusive disease. Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 898 27/02/19 4:13 PM 899 ARTERIAL DISEASECHAPTER 23 Table 23-2 Grading scales for peripheral pulses TRADITIONAL SCALE BASIC SCALE 4+ Normal 2+ Normal 3+ Slightly reduced 1+ Diminished 2+ Markedly reduced 0 Absent 1+ Barely palpable patient is prompted to sleep with their foot hanging off one side of the bed to increase the hydrostatic pressure. The Vascular Physical Examination Specific vascular examination should include abdominal aortic palpation, carotid artery examination, and pulse examination of the lower extremity (femoral, popliteal, posterior tibial, and dorsalis pedis arteries). The abdomen should be palpated for an abdominal aortic aneurysm, detected as an expansile pulse above the level of the umbilicus. It should also be examined for the presence of bruits. Because the aorta typically divides at the level of the umbilicus, an aortic aneurysm is most frequently palpable in the epigastrium. In thin individuals, a normal aortic pulsation is palpable, while in obese patients, even large aor- tic aneurysms may not be detectable. Suspicion of a clinically enlarged aorta should lead to the performance of an ultrasound scan for a more accurate definition of aortic diameter. The carotids should be auscultated for the presence of bruits, although there is a higher correlation with coronary artery disease than underlying carotid stenosis. A bruit at the angle of the mandible is a significant finding, leading to follow- up duplex scanning. The differential diagnosis is a transmitted murmur from a sclerotic or stenotic aortic valve. The carotid is palpable deep to the sternocleidomastoid muscle in the neck. Palpation, however, should be gentle and rarely yields clinically useful information. Upper extremity examination is necessary when an arte- riovenous graft is to be inserted in patients who have symptoms of arm pain with exercise. Thoracic outlet syndrome (TOS) can result in occlusion or aneurysm formation of the subclavian artery. Distal embolization is a manifestation of TOS; conse- quently, the fin gers sho uld be exa mined for sig ns of isc hemia and ulceration. The axillary artery enters the limb below the middle of the clavicle, where it can be palpated in thin patients. It is usually easily palpable in the axilla and medial upper arm. The brachial artery is most easily located at the antecubital fossa immediately medial to the biceps tendon. The radial artery is palpable at the wrist anterior to the radius. For lower extremity vascular examination, the femoral pulse is usually palpable midway between the anterior supe- rior iliac spine and the pubic tubercle. The popliteal artery is palpated in the popliteal fossa with the knee flexed to 45° and the foot supported on th e ex amination ta ble to re lax th e ca lf muscles. Palpation of the popliteal artery is a bimanual tech- nique. Both thumbs are placed on the tibial tuberosity anteriorly, and the fingers are placed into the popliteal fossa between the two heads of the gastrocnemius muscle. The popliteal artery is palpated by compressing it against the posterior aspect of the tibia just below the knee. The posterior tibial pulse is detected by palpation 2 cm posterior to the medial malleolus. The dorsa- lis pedis is detected 1 cm lateral to the hallucis longus extensor tendon, which dorsiflexes the great toe and is clearly visible on the dorsum of the foot. Pulses can be grade d using eithe r the traditional four-point scale or the basic two-point scale system (Table 23-2). The foot should also be carefully examined for pallor on elevation and rubor on dependency, as these findings are indicative of chronic ischemia. Note should also be made of nail changes and loss of hair. Ulceration and other findings specific to disease states are described in relevant sections later in this chapter. After reconstructive vascular surgery, the graft may be available for examination, depending on its type and course. The in situ lower extremity graft runs in the subcutaneous fat and can be palpated along most of its length. A change in pulse quality, aneurysmal enlargement, or a new bruit should be care- fully noted. Axillofemoral grafts, femoral-to-femoral grafts, and arteriovenous access grafts can usually be easily palpated as well. Noninvasive Diagnostic Evaluation of the Vascular Patient Ankle-Brachial Index. There is increasing interest in the use of the ankle-brachial index (ABI) to evaluate patients at risk for cardiovascular events. An ABI less than 0.9 correlates with increased risk of myocardial infarction and indicates significant, although perhaps asymptomatic, underlying peripheral vascular disease. The ABI is determined in the following ways. <span style="color:#e2e8f0">Blood</span> pressure is measured in both upper extremities using the high- est systolic blood pressure as the denominator for the ABI. The ankle pressure is determined by placing a blood pressure cuff above the ankle and measuring the return to flow of the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries using a pencil Doppler probe over each artery. The ratio of the systolic pressure in each ves- sel divided by the highest arm systolic pressure can be used to express the ABI in both the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries (Fig. 23-1). Normal is more than 1. Patients with clau- dication typically have an ABI in the 0.5 to 0.7 range, and those with rest pain are in the 0.3 to 0.5 range. Those with gangrene have an ABI of less than 0.3. These ranges can vary depending on the degree of compressibility of the vessel. The test is less reliable in patients with heavily calcified vessels. Due to non- compressibility, some patients, such as diabetics and those with end-stage renal disease, may have ABI ≥1.40 and require addi- tional noninvasive diagnostic testing to evaluate for peripheral artery disease. Alternative tests include toe-brachial pressures, pulse volume recordings, transcutaneous oxygen measurements, or vascular imaging (duplex ultrasound). Segmental Limb Pressures. By placing serial blood pressure cuffs down the lowe r extr emity and then meas uring the pres sure with a Doppler probe as flow returns to the artery below the cuff, it is possible to de termine se gmental pr essures do wn the leg. This data can then be used to infer the level of the occlu- sion. The systolic pressure at each level is expressed as a ratio, with the highest systolic pressure in the upper extremities as the denominator. Normal segmental pressures commonly show high thigh pressures 20 mmHg or greater in comparison to the brachial artery pressures. The low thigh pressure should be equivalent to brachial pressures. Subsequent pressures should fall by no more than 10 mmHg at each level. A pressure gradient of 20 mmHg between two subsequent levels is usually indica- tive of occlusive disease at that level. The most frequently used index is the ratio of the ankle pressure to the brachial pressure, Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 899 27/02/19 4:13 PM

This report details the early and 1-year outcomes of an international prospective study comparing valve-sparing and valve-replacing root operations in patients with Marfan syndrome. Although patients in both groups had excellent early outcomes, those who underwent valve-sparing procedures had a higher incidence of late aortic valve regur- gitation, raising concerns about long-term durability.

Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 897 27/02/19 4:13 PM 898 Table 23-1 Pertinent elements in vascular history • History of stroke or transient ischemic attack • History of coronary artery disease, including previous myocardial infarction and angina • History of peripheral arterial disease • History of diabetes • History of hypertension • History of tobacco use • History of hyperlipidemia GENERAL APPROACH TO THE VASCULAR PATIENT Since the vascular system involves every organ system in our body, the symptoms of vascular disease are as varied as those encountered in any medical specialty. Lack of adequate blood supply to target organs typically presents with pain, for exam- ple, calf pain with lower extremity claudication, postprandial abdominal pain from mesenteric ischemia, and arm pain with axillo-subclavian arterial occlusion. In contrast, stroke and tran- sient ischemic attack (TIA) are the presenting symptoms from middle cerebral embolization as a consequence of a stenosed internal carotid artery. The pain syndrome of arterial disease is usually divided clinically into acute and chronic types, with all shades of severity between the two extremes. Sudden onset of pain can indicate complete occlusion of a critical vessel, leading to mo re se vere pa in an d cr itical is chemia in th e ta rget organ, resulting in lower limb gangrene or intestinal infarction. Chronic pain results from a slower, more progressive atheroscle- rotic occlusion, whic h can be tota lly or par tially comp ensated by developing collateral vessels. Acute on chronic is another pain pattern in which a patient most likely has an underlying arterial stenosis that suddenly occludes, for example, the patient with a history of calf claudication who now presents with sudden, severe acute limb-threatening ischemia. The clinician should always try to understand and relate the clinical manifestations to the underlying pathologic process. The Vascular History Appropriate history should be focused based on the present- ing symptoms re lated to th e va scular sy stem (T able 23 -1). Of particular importance in the previous medical history is noting prior vascular interventions (endovascular or open surgical), and all vascular patients should have inquiry made about their prior cardiac history an d cu rrent ca rdiac sy mptoms. Ap proximately 30% of vascular patients will be diabetic. A history of prior and current smoking status should be noted. The patient with carotid disease in most cases is com- pletely asymptomatic, having been referred based on the find- ing of a cervical bruit or duplex finding of stenosis. Symptoms of carotid territory TIAs include transient monocular blindness (amaurosis), contralateral weakness or numbness, and dys- phasia. Symptoms persisting longer than 24 hours constitute a stroke. In contrast, the patient with chronic mesenteric isch- emia is likely to present with postprandial abdominal pain and weight loss. The patient fears eating because of the pain, avoids food, and loses weight. It is very unlikely that a patient with abdominal pain who has not lost weight has chronic mesenteric ischemia. The patient with lower extremity pain on ambulation has intermittent claudication that occurs in certain muscle groups; for example, calf pain upon exercise usually reflects superficial femoral artery disease, while pain in the buttocks reflects iliac disease. In most cases, the pain manifests in one muscle group below the level of the affected artery, occurs only with exercise, and is relieved with rest only to recur at the same location, hence the term “window gazer’s disease.” Rest pain (a manifestation of severe underlying occlusive disease) is constant and occurs in the foot (not the muscle groups), typically at the metatarso- phalangeal junction, and is relieved by dependency. Often the Key Points 1 Carotid intervention as a preventive strategy should be per- formed in patients with 60% or greater symptomatic internal carotid artery stenosis and those with 80% or greater asymp- tomatic internal carotid artery stenosis. Carotid intervention for asymptomatic stenosis betwee n 60% and 79% remain s controversial. The modality of carotid intervention—carotid endarterectomy versus carotid stenting—remains contro- versial; currently, carotid endarterectomy appears to be associated with lower stroke rate with long term durability, whereas carotid stenting is more suitable under certain chal- lenging anatomic or physiologic conditions. 2 Abdominal aortic aneurysms should be repaired when the risk of ru pture, de termined ma inly by an eurysm si ze, exceeds the risk of death due to perioperative complications or concurrent illness. Endovascular repair is associated with less perioperative morbidity and mortality compared with open reconstruction and is preferred in patients with suitable anatomic morphology for stent-graft placement. 3 Treatment objectives for sym ptomatic mes enteric isc hemia are to improve quality of life and prevent bowel infarc- tion. Endovascular intervention with stenting has similar treatment efficacy comparative with less perioperative mor- bidity compared to open mesenteric bypass. Surgical recon- struction has a proven durability and patency rate compared with endovascular intervention. 4 Aortoiliac occlusive disease can be treated with either endovascular me ans or op en re construction, de pending on patient risk stratification, occlusion characteristics, and symptomatology. 5 Claudication is a marker of extensive atherosclerosis and is mainly managed with risk factor modification and pharma- cotherapy. Only 5% of patients with claudication will need intervention because of disabling extremity pain. The 5-year mortality of a patient with claudication approaches 30%. Patients with rest pain or tissue loss need expeditious evalu- ation and vascular reconstruction to ameliorate the severe extremity pain an d pr event li mb lo ss. En dovascular in terven- tion is preferred as the first line of therapy for lower extrem- ity occlusive disease, whereas bypass reconstruction should be considered in failed endovascu lar therapy or long seg- ment femoropopliteal occlusive disease. Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 898 27/02/19 4:13 PM 899 ARTERIAL DISEASECHAPTER 23 Table 23-2 Grading scales for peripheral pulses TRADITIONAL SCALE BASIC SCALE 4+ Normal 2+ Normal 3+ Slightly reduced 1+ Diminished 2+ Markedly reduced 0 Absent 1+ Barely palpable patient is prompted to sleep with their foot hanging off one side of the bed to increase the hydrostatic pressure. The Vascular Physical Examination Specific vascular examination should include abdominal aortic palpation, carotid artery examination, and pulse examination of the lower extremity (femoral, popliteal, posterior tibial, and dorsalis pedis arteries). The abdomen should be palpated for an abdominal aortic aneurysm, detected as an expansile pulse above the level of the umbilicus. It should also be examined for the presence of bruits. Because the aorta typically divides at the level of the umbilicus, an aortic aneurysm is most frequently palpable in the epigastrium. In thin individuals, a normal aortic pulsation is palpable, while in obese patients, even large aor- tic aneurysms may not be detectable. Suspicion of a clinically enlarged aorta should lead to the performance of an ultrasound scan for a more accurate definition of aortic diameter. The carotids should be auscultated for the presence of bruits, although there is a higher correlation with coronary artery disease than underlying carotid stenosis. A bruit at the angle of the mandible is a significant finding, leading to follow- up duplex scanning. The differential diagnosis is a transmitted murmur from a sclerotic or stenotic aortic valve. The carotid is palpable deep to the sternocleidomastoid muscle in the neck. Palpation, however, should be gentle and rarely yields clinically useful information. Upper extremity examination is necessary when an arte- riovenous graft is to be inserted in patients who have symptoms of arm pain with exercise. Thoracic outlet syndrome (TOS) can result in occlusion or aneurysm formation of the subclavian artery. Distal embolization is a manifestation of TOS; conse- quently, the fin gers sho uld be exa mined for sig ns of isc hemia and ulceration. The axillary artery enters the limb below the middle of the clavicle, where it can be palpated in thin patients. It is usually easily palpable in the axilla and medial upper arm. The brachial artery is most easily located at the antecubital fossa immediately medial to the biceps tendon. The radial artery is palpable at the wrist anterior to the radius. For lower extremity vascular examination, the femoral pulse is usually palpable midway between the anterior supe- rior iliac spine and the pubic tubercle. The popliteal artery is palpated in the popliteal fossa with the knee flexed to 45° and the foot supported on th e ex amination ta ble to re lax th e ca lf muscles. Palpation of the popliteal artery is a bimanual tech- nique. Both thumbs are placed on the tibial tuberosity anteriorly, and the fingers are placed into the popliteal fossa between the two heads of the gastrocnemius muscle. The popliteal artery is palpated by compressing it against the posterior aspect of the tibia just below the knee. The posterior tibial pulse is detected by palpation 2 cm posterior to the medial malleolus. The dorsa- lis pedis is detected 1 cm lateral to the hallucis longus extensor tendon, which dorsiflexes the great toe and is clearly visible on the dorsum of the foot. Pulses can be grade d using eithe r the traditional four-point scale or the basic two-point scale system (Table 23-2). The foot should also be carefully examined for pallor on elevation and rubor on dependency, as these findings are indicative of chronic ischemia. Note should also be made of nail changes and loss of hair. Ulceration and other findings specific to disease states are described in relevant sections later in this chapter. After reconstructive vascular surgery, the graft may be available for examination, depending on its type and course. The in situ lower extremity graft runs in the subcutaneous fat and can be palpated along most of its length. A change in pulse quality, aneurysmal enlargement, or a new bruit should be care- fully noted. Axillofemoral grafts, femoral-to-femoral grafts, and arteriovenous access grafts can usually be easily palpated as well. Noninvasive Diagnostic Evaluation of the Vascular Patient Ankle-Brachial Index. There is increasing interest in the use of the ankle-brachial index (ABI) to evaluate patients at risk for cardiovascular events. An ABI less than 0.9 correlates with increased risk of myocardial infarction and indicates significant, although perhaps asymptomatic, underlying peripheral vascular disease. The ABI is determined in the following ways. <span style="color:#e2e8f0">Blood</span> pressure is measured in both upper extremities using the high- est systolic blood pressure as the denominator for the ABI. The ankle pressure is determined by placing a blood pressure cuff above the ankle and measuring the return to flow of the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries using a pencil Doppler probe over each artery. The ratio of the systolic pressure in each ves- sel divided by the highest arm systolic pressure can be used to express the ABI in both the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries (Fig. 23-1). Normal is more than 1. Patients with clau- dication typically have an ABI in the 0.5 to 0.7 range, and those with rest pain are in the 0.3 to 0.5 range. Those with gangrene have an ABI of less than 0.3. These ranges can vary depending on the degree of compressibility of the vessel. The test is less reliable in patients with heavily calcified vessels. Due to non- compressibility, some patients, such as diabetics and those with end-stage renal disease, may have ABI ≥1.40 and require addi- tional noninvasive diagnostic testing to evaluate for peripheral artery disease. Alternative tests include toe-brachial pressures, pulse volume recordings, transcutaneous oxygen measurements, or vascular imaging (duplex ultrasound). Segmental Limb Pressures. By placing serial blood pressure cuffs down the lowe r extr emity and then meas uring the pres sure with a Doppler probe as flow returns to the artery below the cuff, it is possible to de termine se gmental pr essures do wn the leg. This data can then be used to infer the level of the occlu- sion. The systolic pressure at each level is expressed as a ratio, with the highest systolic pressure in the upper extremities as the denominator. Normal segmental pressures commonly show high thigh pressures 20 mmHg or greater in comparison to the brachial artery pressures. The low thigh pressure should be equivalent to brachial pressures. Subsequent pressures should fall by no more than 10 mmHg at each level. A pressure gradient of 20 mmHg between two subsequent levels is usually indica- tive of occlusive disease at that level. The most frequently used index is the ratio of the ankle pressure to the brachial pressure, Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 899 27/02/19 4:13 PM

This report details the early and 1-year outcomes of an international prospective study comparing valve-sparing and valve-replacing root operations in patients with Marfan syndrome. Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 897 27/02/19 4:13 PM 898 Table 23-1 Pertinent elements in vascular history • History of stroke or transient ischemic attack • History of coronary artery disease, including previous myocardial infarction and angina • History of peripheral arterial disease • History of diabetes • History of hypertension • History of tobacco use • History of hyperlipidemia GENERAL APPROACH TO THE VASCULAR PATIENT Since the vascular system involves every organ system in our body, the symptoms of vascular disease are as varied as those encountered in any medical specialty. Lack of adequate blood supply to target organs typically presents with pain, for exam- ple, calf pain with lower extremity claudication, postprandial abdominal pain from mesenteric ischemia, and arm pain with axillo-subclavian arterial occlusion. In contrast, stroke and tran- sient ischemic attack (TIA) are the presenting symptoms from middle cerebral embolization as a consequence of a stenosed internal carotid artery. The pain syndrome of arterial disease is usually divided clinically into acute and chronic types, with all shades of severity between the two extremes. Sudden onset of pain can indicate complete occlusion of a critical vessel, leading to mo re se vere pa in an d cr itical is chemia in th e ta rget organ, resulting in lower limb gangrene or intestinal infarction. Chronic pain results from a slower, more progressive atheroscle- rotic occlusion, whic h can be tota lly or par tially comp ensated by developing collateral vessels. Acute on chronic is another pain pattern in which a patient most likely has an underlying arterial stenosis that suddenly occludes, for example, the patient with a history of calf claudication who now presents with sudden, severe acute limb-threatening ischemia. The clinician should always try to understand and relate the clinical manifestations to the underlying pathologic process. The Vascular History Appropriate history should be focused based on the present- ing symptoms re lated to th e va scular sy stem (T able 23 -1). Of particular importance in the previous medical history is noting prior vascular interventions (endovascular or open surgical), and all vascular patients should have inquiry made about their prior cardiac history an d cu rrent ca rdiac sy mptoms. Ap proximately 30% of vascular patients will be diabetic. A history of prior and current smoking status should be noted. The patient with carotid disease in most cases is com- pletely asymptomatic, having been referred based on the find- ing of a cervical bruit or duplex finding of stenosis. Symptoms of carotid territory TIAs include transient monocular blindness (amaurosis), contralateral weakness or numbness, and dys- phasia. Symptoms persisting longer than 24 hours constitute a stroke. In contrast, the patient with chronic mesenteric isch- emia is likely to present with postprandial abdominal pain and weight loss. The patient fears eating because of the pain, avoids food, and loses weight. It is very unlikely that a patient with abdominal pain who has not lost weight has chronic mesenteric ischemia. The patient with lower extremity pain on ambulation has intermittent claudication that occurs in certain muscle groups; for example, calf pain upon exercise usually reflects superficial femoral artery disease, while pain in the buttocks reflects iliac disease. In most cases, the pain manifests in one muscle group below the level of the affected artery, occurs only with exercise, and is relieved with rest only to recur at the same location, hence the term “window gazer’s disease.” Rest pain (a manifestation of severe underlying occlusive disease) is constant and occurs in the foot (not the muscle groups), typically at the metatarso- phalangeal junction, and is relieved by dependency. Often the Key Points 1 Carotid intervention as a preventive strategy should be per- formed in patients with 60% or greater symptomatic internal carotid artery stenosis and those with 80% or greater asymp- tomatic internal carotid artery stenosis. Carotid intervention for asymptomatic stenosis betwee n 60% and 79% remain s controversial. The modality of carotid intervention—carotid endarterectomy versus carotid stenting—remains contro- versial; currently, carotid endarterectomy appears to be associated with lower stroke rate with long term durability, whereas carotid stenting is more suitable under certain chal- lenging anatomic or physiologic conditions. 2 Abdominal aortic aneurysms should be repaired when the risk of ru pture, de termined ma inly by an eurysm si ze, exceeds the risk of death due to perioperative complications or concurrent illness. Endovascular repair is associated with less perioperative morbidity and mortality compared with open reconstruction and is preferred in patients with suitable anatomic morphology for stent-graft placement. 3 Treatment objectives for sym ptomatic mes enteric isc hemia are to improve quality of life and prevent bowel infarc- tion. Endovascular intervention with stenting has similar treatment efficacy comparative with less perioperative mor- bidity compared to open mesenteric bypass. Surgical recon- struction has a proven durability and patency rate compared with endovascular intervention. 4 Aortoiliac occlusive disease can be treated with either endovascular me ans or op en re construction, de pending on patient risk stratification, occlusion characteristics, and symptomatology. 5 Claudication is a marker of extensive atherosclerosis and is mainly managed with risk factor modification and pharma- cotherapy. Only 5% of patients with claudication will need intervention because of disabling extremity pain. The 5-year mortality of a patient with claudication approaches 30%. Patients with rest pain or tissue loss need expeditious evalu- ation and vascular reconstruction to ameliorate the severe extremity pain an d pr event li mb lo ss. En dovascular in terven- tion is preferred as the first line of therapy for lower extrem- ity occlusive disease, whereas bypass reconstruction should be considered in failed endovascu lar therapy or long seg- ment femoropopliteal occlusive disease. Brunicardi_Ch23_p<span style="color:#f87171">0897</span>-p<span style="color:#f87171">0980</span>.indd 898 27/02/19 4:13 PM 899 ARTERIAL DISEASECHAPTER 23 Table 23-2 Grading scales for peripheral pulses TRADITIONAL SCALE BASIC SCALE 4+ Normal 2+ Normal 3+ Slightly reduced 1+ Diminished 2+ Markedly reduced 0 Absent 1+ Barely palpable patient is prompted to sleep with their foot hanging off one side of the bed to increase the hydrostatic pressure. The Vascular Physical Examination Specific vascular examination should include abdominal aortic palpation, carotid artery examination, and pulse examination of the lower extremity (femoral, popliteal, posterior tibial, and dorsalis pedis arteries). The abdomen should be palpated for an abdominal aortic aneurysm, detected as an expansile pulse above the level of the umbilicus. It should also be examined for the presence of bruits. Because the aorta typically divides at the level of the umbilicus, an aortic aneurysm is most frequently palpable in the epigastrium. In thin individuals, a normal aortic pulsation is palpable, while in obese patients, even large aor- tic aneurysms may not be detectable. Suspicion of a clinically enlarged aorta should lead to the performance of an ultrasound scan for a more accurate definition of aortic diameter. The carotids should be auscultated for the presence of bruits, although there is a higher correlation with coronary artery disease than underlying carotid stenosis. A bruit at the angle of the mandible is a significant finding, leading to follow- up duplex scanning. The differential diagnosis is a transmitted murmur from a sclerotic or stenotic aortic valve. The carotid is palpable deep to the sternocleidomastoid muscle in the neck. Palpation, however, should be gentle and rarely yields clinically useful information. Upper extremity examination is necessary when an arte- riovenous graft is to be inserted in patients who have symptoms of arm pain with exercise. Thoracic outlet syndrome (TOS) can result in occlusion or aneurysm formation of the subclavian artery. Distal embolization is a manifestation of TOS; conse- quently, the fin gers sho uld be exa mined for sig ns of isc hemia and ulceration. The axillary artery enters the limb below the middle of the clavicle, where it can be palpated in thin patients. It is usually easily palpable in the axilla and medial upper arm. The brachial artery is most easily located at the antecubital fossa immediately medial to the biceps tendon. The radial artery is palpable at the wrist anterior to the radius. For lower extremity vascular examination, the femoral pulse is usually palpable midway between the anterior supe- rior iliac spine and the pubic tubercle. The popliteal artery is palpated in the popliteal fossa with the knee flexed to 45° and the foot supported on th e ex amination ta ble to re lax th e ca lf muscles. Palpation of the popliteal artery is a bimanual tech- nique. Both thumbs are placed on the tibial tuberosity anteriorly, and the fingers are placed into the popliteal fossa between the two heads of the gastrocnemius muscle. The popliteal artery is palpated by compressing it against the posterior aspect of the tibia just below the knee. The posterior tibial pulse is detected by palpation 2 cm posterior to the medial malleolus. The dorsa- lis pedis is detected 1 cm lateral to the hallucis longus extensor tendon, which dorsiflexes the great toe and is clearly visible on the dorsum of the foot. Pulses can be grade d using eithe r the traditional four-point scale or the basic two-point scale system (Table 23-2). The foot should also be carefully examined for pallor on elevation and rubor on dependency, as these findings are indicative of chronic ischemia. Note should also be made of nail changes and loss of hair. Ulceration and other findings specific to disease states are described in relevant sections later in this chapter. After reconstructive vascular surgery, the graft may be available for examination, depending on its type and course. The in situ lower extremity graft runs in the subcutaneous fat and can be palpated along most of its length. A change in pulse quality, aneurysmal enlargement, or a new bruit should be care- fully noted. Axillofemoral grafts, femoral-to-femoral grafts, and arteriovenous access grafts can usually be easily palpated as well. Noninvasive Diagnostic Evaluation of the Vascular Patient Ankle-Brachial Index. There is increasing interest in the use of the ankle-brachial index (ABI) to evaluate patients at risk for cardiovascular events. An ABI less than 0.9 correlates with increased risk of myocardial infarction and indicates significant, although perhaps asymptomatic, underlying peripheral vascular disease. The ABI is determined in the following ways. <span style="color:#e2e8f0">Blood</span> pressure is measured in both upper extremities using the high- est systolic blood pressure as the denominator for the ABI. The ankle pressure is determined by placing a blood pressure cuff above the ankle and measuring the return to flow of the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries using a pencil Doppler probe over each artery. The ratio of the systolic pressure in each ves- sel divided by the highest arm systolic pressure can be used to express the ABI in both the posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries (Fig. 23-1). Normal is more than 1. Patients with clau- dication typically have an ABI in the 0.5 to 0.7 range, and those with rest pain are in the 0.3 to 0.5 range. Those with gangrene have an ABI of less than 0.3. These ranges can vary depending on the degree of compressibility of the vessel. The test is less reliable in patients with heavily calcified vessels. Due to non- compressibility, some patients, such as diabetics and those with end-stage renal disease, may have ABI ≥1.40 and require addi- tional noninvasive diagnostic testing to evaluate for peripheral artery disease. Alternative tests include toe-brachial pressures, pulse volume recordings, transcutaneous oxygen measurements, or vascular imaging (duplex ultrasound). Segmental Limb Pressures. By placing serial blood pressure cuffs down the lowe r extr emity and then meas uring the pres sure with a Doppler probe as flow returns to the artery below the cuff, it is possible to de termine se gmental pr essures do wn the leg. This data can then be used to infer the level of the occlu- sion. The systolic pressure at each level is expressed as a ratio, with the highest systolic pressure in the upper extremities as the denominator. Normal segmental pressures commonly show high thigh pressures 20 mmHg or greater in comparison to the brachial artery pressures. The low thigh pressure should be equivalent to brachial pressures. Subsequent pressures should fall by no more than 10 mmHg at each level. A pressure gradient of 20 mmHg between two subsequent levels is usually indica- tive of occlusive disease at that level.
